The main places where Spanish is spoken are Spain, of course, and Latin America. But there's no Latin Asia, right? Well, actually, there is. It's just very small. I'm talking about the Spanish-sp speakaking community of the Philippines, or what's left of it. Spanish was the official language in the Philippines for more than three centuries of Spanish rule and remained one of the official languages until 1987. While Spanish never became a majority language of the Philippines, it was on its way to doing so until the Americans asserted English as the official language. Spanish contact with the Philippines began in 1521 when Ferdinand Mellin, a Portuguese explorer sailing for Spain, landed in the Visayas region. He claimed the islands for Spain but was killed before he managed to establish a colony. In 1543, another Spanish explorer, Ru Lopez de Valobos, made a similar failed attempt, but he left the lasting impact, the name of the country. He named the islands of Lee and Samar Lassis Filipinas after the Spanish prince Felipe II who later became king and that name was later applied to the entire archipelago. So the name of the Philippines comes from the name of the Spanish king Felipe and its English equivalent Philip. But the real colonization of the Philippines began in 1565 when another explorer, Miguel Lopez degaspi, successfully established a colony and declared the Philippine Islands part of the vice royalty of New Spain, which was based in Mexico. The Philippine Islands and Mexico were connected through the Gallian trade route between Manila and Aapulco. And for 256 years, the Philippines had extensive contact with New Spain. After Mexico gained its independence in 1821, the Philippines came under the direct rule of Spain until 1898 when they lost the Philippines to the USA in the SpanishAmerican War. For most of the Spanish colonial period, the Spanish language didn't spread throughout the Philippines the way it did in Latin America. It was spoken by the colonial administration and their families, some foreignb born traders, Catholic clergy who were mainly from Spain and Latin America, and the native Filipino elite and local leaders. It was also learned to varying extents by native sailors, port workers, and traders. But Spanish language was not emphasized for the general population, and there was essentially no public education system. Local education was provided by the Catholic Church who focused on literacy in local languages and on religion. And they taught in local languages, not Spanish, though they did throw in some Spanish words for religious concepts and other things. There was no local word for [music] words like >> Dios >> meaning God, >> meaning church and >> truth >> meaning cross. So outside of the urban elite, most people learned only isolated Spanish words. But in 1863, Spain issued a decree mandating a public primary education system in the Philippines, the first public school system in Asia, and Spanish was made the language of instruction. It would have been hard to enact that because most teachers themselves didn't know Spanish fluently and a lot of them were probably learning it as they taught it. But it certainly led to at least some increase in Spanish ability amongst the general population. In 1908, a member of the Filipino Assembly, Luciano de la Rosa, estimated that around 10% spoke Spanish as their primary language, but an additional 60% spoke it as a second language. And in a report to US President Woodro Wilson in 1916, a US official Henry J. Ford seemed to say that Spanish was still quite widely spoken even after years of American rule. He wrote, "Spanish is everywhere the language of business and social intercourse. In order for anyone to obtain prompt service from anyone, Spanish turns out to be more useful than English. And outside of Manila, it's almost indispensable. The [music] Americans who travel around all the islands customarily use it. In the census conducted 2 years later in 1918, 7.3% of the population claimed to be able to speak Spanish and 8.7% English. But it's clearly stated that those numbers only included people at least 10 years of age who were literate. Since less than half of Filipinos were literate in any language, the percentage of people who could speak Spanish might have been quite a bit higher, even if they weren't fluent. Most newspapers were still in Spanish, and most printed literature was still in Spanish. And in certain urban areas like Manila, Spanish was one of the major languages of daily life. So, it's clear that Spanish would have become the major lingua frana of the Philippines. But under American rule, it lost its momentum and declined as English gained dominance. ## 6. WASEI EIGO - English Made in Japan
**Channel:** Langfocus | **Views:** 38K | **Date:** 4 months ago | **Duration:** 10:06 | **ID:** 9f3gH0-kVNI
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=9f3gH0-kVNI

### Transcript:
The core of the Japanese language 
consists of native Japanese words,   but Japanese has a long history of adopting words 
from other languages too - primarily from Chinese,   but later from Portuguese, Dutch, French, 
German, and in recent decades - English!
  But these words aren’t always used in the same 
way in Japanese as they are in the original   language. There are lots of English words used 
in Japanese with a somewhat different meaning,   and there are numerous compound words and 
phrases that were coined from English words,   but aren’t actually used in English. These are 
called WASEI EIGO - which translates to “English   made in Japan”. Some of these words and phrases 
sound a bit funny to native speakers of English,   and can result in some misunderstandings.
One of the most famous examples is:
  マンション manshon - from English “mansion”. 
This means an “apartment”. So if a Japanese   person tells you they live in a mansion - 
they’re probably not bragging about their   huge expensive house. They might actually 
live in a little shoebox apartment. 
  サラリーマン sararīman - from the English 
words “salary man”. This means a male   office worker. The female counterpart is オーエル 
ōeru (from O.L. - short for Office Lady).
  リストラ risutora - from “restructuring”. This 
means “being laid off” (due to restructuring,   as opposed to being fired for performance 
reasons). For example: お父さんは去年リストラされた means   “My father was laid off last year”. So リストラされた 
means “was laid off”, not “was restructured”.
  The average person in Japan becomes a sararīman 
or ōeru, but some people dream of becoming a: 
  タレント tarento - from English “talent”. This is 
a celebrity or tv personality, usually someone   who’s famous for being famous - they don’t really 
have any real claim to fame. They often appear on   variety shows in Japan - which are simply for 
entertainment and have no consistent theme.
  チャームポイント chāmu pointo - from the phrase “charm 
point”. This means “Your most attractive feature”.   I’ve seen some variety shows if which they 
ask female tarento “チャームポイントはなんですか - “What’s   your most attractive feature?” Based on 
the word “charm” you might expect them   to give a personality trait, like kindness or 
generosity. But instead it’s usually something   physical like “my eyes”, “my cheeks”, etc.
Sometimes people in Japan   describe their personality as:
マイペース mai pēsu - from the words “my pace”.   This means “doing things at one’s own pace”. But 
it’s often used to mean something like “laid back”   or “chill” when describing someone’s character.
テンション tenshon - from English “tension”. - This   means energy level or mood. テンションが高い 
(or ハイテンション hai tenshon) means “high   energy or in a good mood”. It does 
NOT mean tense or stressed.
  When are people are stressed they 
sometimes go shopping to boost   their mood. Especially if there’s a:
バーゲン bāgen - from English “bargain”. This   means a “sale” (like when a shop temporarily 
lowers prices). Of course this is different   from the word bargain in English, which means 
“a good deal” for a specific purchase.
  When you go shopping downtown or at the 
mall, you might also stop for a meal at   a restaurant - and it might be a:
バイキング baikingu. This comes from English   “Viking”. It means “buffet” - like a self serve 
all-you-can-eat meal. You can feast like a   Viking! The concept of an all-you-can-eat buffet 
didn’t really exist in Japan until the 1950s.   The first buffet opened at the Imperial Hotel 
in Tokyo, at the time when the Kurt Douglas   movie “The Vikings” was in theatres - and the 
images of Vikings feasting on food inspired the   name baikingu for the buffet. And as buffets 
became more widespread, the word did too.
  A typical baikingu has a wide variety of foods, 
and I’d be surprised if they didn’t have:
  フライドポテト furaido poteto - from English 
“fried potato”. This means “french fries”. 
  If it’s a morning buffet, they might have:
ホットケーキ hotto kēki - from English hot cake. What   is that, a cake served hot? 
No, it means “pancake”.
  If you’re lucky they might have:
アメリカンドッグ amerikan doggu - from English   “American dog”. This means “corndog”. I hereby 
propose that we re-borrow this term from Japanese   and call them American dogs from now on!
Japanese buffets are usually decent quality,   but once a friend of mine   found a fly in his food, so he made a:
クレーム kurēmu. This comes from the English word   “claim” - but it means “complaint”.
When I go out to eat I usually take   the train because I’m a:
ペーパードライバー pēpā doraibā. This   comes from English “paper driver”. It means 
someone who has a driver’s license but hasn’t   driven for a long time, so they’re rusty or 
don’t feel confident driving again. There are   a lot of paper drivers in Japan, because public 
transportation is so good and efficient that you   can get away with not driving for a long time.
ドライブ doraibu - from English “drive”. This means   the act of “driving for fun” or “going for a 
drive”. When you ask Japanese speakers what   they like to do for fun, sometimes they say 
ドライブ - they like driving. That usually means   making day trips and enjoying the 
destination as well as the scenery   on the way, so it’s not just the driving.
ハンドル handoru - from the English word “handle”.   This means a “car steering wheel”.
ハンドルキーパー handoru kīpā - from English   “handle keeper”. This means “designated driver” 
- someone who avoids drinking alcohol so they can   drive their friends who are drinking.
If you plan on being a handoru kīpā you   probably need to have:
マイカー maikā - from English “my car”.   But this doesn’t exactly mean “MY car”. In wasei 
eigo words like this, マイ takes on the meaning of   “individual” or “personally owned”, or as I like 
to think of it: “one’s own” as in “one’s own car”.   It’s not really a conversational phrase, but it 
appears in ads, signs, surveys, lists of rules,   etc. You might see an ad for マイカー保険 - “personal 
car insurance.” ; or your university’s rules   might say: マイカー通学はできません - “Commuting to 
school by private car is not allowed.”
  As a side note, Japan’s new national ID 
cards are called マイナンバーカード which means   “individual number card” or “personal 
number card”. And your personal   identification number is called 
マイナンバー - from English “my number”. 
  Another kind of car is a:
ベビーカー bebī kā - from English “baby   car”. This means “stroller”. In my opinion this 
is one of the most awesome wasei eigo words.
  Another awesome one:
ジェットコースター jetto kōsutā - from “jet coaster”.   This means “roller coaster”. But “jet coaster” 
is a way better name for it. I think we should   re-borrow that word from Japanese into English 
and start using it immediately. “Dad can we go on   the roller coaster?” “Nah, that sounds boring.”
“Can we go on the JET COASTER?” “Oh, hell yeah,   let’s f**king go!”
When you ride a jet coaster,   you’d better hold onto your belongings so 
they don’t fall or go flying through the air,   like your cellphone, sunglasses, or:
ピアス piasu (from English pierce) - This   means “earring”. There is also another word イヤリング 
iyaringu, but this refers specifically to clip-on   earrings. Regular earrings for pierced 
ears are referred to as ピアス piasu.
 
  When you buy items like ピアス piasu, you can 
buy them off the shelf or you buy them: 
  オーダーメイド ōdāmeido - from English “order made”. 
This means “made to order” or “custom-made”.   This suit of mine was オーダーメイド . I tried to 
buy one off the rack but they suggested I   go to the plus size shop…
That kind of hurt my feelings   but my wife was like:
ドンマイ donmai - from English   “Don’t mind”. This means “Don’t worry about 
it”. She was like “You can splurge on a new   custom-made suit as long as I can buy a new”:
ワンピース wanpīsu - from English “one piece”. - You   might assume this means a one piece bathing suit 
but it actually means “a dress”. And the title   of the anime One Piece has nothing to do with this 
meaning. That title refers to a treasure, like the   one single treasure that everyone is searching 
for (something like “One piece of riches”).
  This One Piece character named Brook (ブルック) - 
is a skeleton. In Japanese the word for skeleton   is がいこつ. But the English word is the source of 
a different Japanese word スケルトン - but it doesn’t   refer to a skeleton. It means “transparent” 
or “see-through”. I think that meaning comes   from the idea that you could see someone’s 
skeleton if they were transparent.
  A couple more Wasei Eigo words are:
マザコン mazakon - shortened from マザーコンプレックス - from   English “mother complex”. This refers 
to a person who’s a “mama’s boy”. 
  You might assume the word refers to that 
complex - that mental tendency - but it   actually refers to the person who has it.
There’s also ファザコン fazakon - from “Father   complex”. This refers to a “daddy’s 
girl”, but I don't hear that one much.
  クーラー kūrā - from English “cooler”. This means 
“air coditioner”. In English if you said the   word “cooler” without much context, I would 
probably assume you meant THIS kind of cooler   that you put your drinks or food inside to keep

## 18. How Similar Are ARABIC and HEBREW? (Massive reboot)
**Channel:** Langfocus | **Views:** 375K | **Date:** 1 year ago | **Duration:** 23:38 | **ID:** 4TbbPzJlV2A
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=4TbbPzJlV2A

### Transcript:
in this video I'm going to show you how similar the Arabic and Hebrew languages are I actually made a video about this back in the early days of this channel but like most of my videos from that era it was trash and an important topic like this deserves an upgrade so here we are Arabic and Hebrew are both Semitic languages and developed from the same origin that's why the word for peace is Salam in Arabic and shalom in Hebrew the word for Saturday is as in Arabic and Shabbat in Hebrew which is the source of the English word Sabbath by the way a word used at the end of prayers and so on is Amin in Arabic and am in Hebrew Arabic and Hebrew speakers generally can't understand each other but the similarity in words like this is pretty obvious when you look for it I often see comments on the internet saying that Hebrew developed from Arabic or that Arabic developed from Hebrew but that's not true at this point in the development of Semitic languages they were the same language but then dialects of this language began to gradually diverge into different languages and different parts of the Middle East Hebrew actually died as a daily spoken language around 200 CE and continued to be used mainly in writing especially scholarly religious writing and in Jewish religious practice modern Hebrew resulted from an effort to revive Hebrew as a spoken language beginning in the 1800s as you can imagine it's not exactly the same as older forms of literary Hebrew and lots of new vocabulary for the modern era was added to it most of it was created using Hebrew roots and in some cases Hebrew linguists looked at at modern Arabic vocabulary and created similar words using the equivalent roots and word structure in Hebrew but this was for a fairly small number of words most of the similarities between Arabic and Hebrew are a result of their common Semitic Origins one striking similarity is their similar system of roots and templates both Arabic and Hebrew have a system of word roots usually Roots consisting of three consonant letters you take those three consonant letters and put them into templates that help you create a word a template is like a combination of vowels and affixes whether a prefix suffix or infix for example in Hebrew there's the root CTV and in Arabic you've got CB which both have the core meaning of write let's see some forms of the verb meaning to many languages are now spoken there and dialects closely related to Yuba are spoken in Southeastern Benin and parts of Togo along with Yuba proper these dialects are part of the yoid language group which form part of the Niger Congo language family yoid includes igala IDI which splits into itaki the Ed dialects spoken mainly in Benin and Togo and yua dialects Ed dialects are sometimes considered Yuba dialects as they are very closely related and their speakers identify as ethnic Yuba people Yuba proper has many dialects but their standard Yuba which serves as the formal and written form of the language and a bridge between those dialects yua dialects and related yoid languages are spoken throughout Yuba land but all the yua people see IL as the origin of their civilization during the Eep Kingdom which was dominant between the year 1000 and 1420 in the yuat tradition IL is thought to have been founded by odua a yua king thought to be divine but Yuba civilization didn't consist of just a single Yuba political entity there were numerous Yoda city states that spoke Yoda dialects or related Yobo dialects including oo the oo Empire surpassed IL and became the main yua power from 1600 to around 1800 ad until internal conflict led to its downfall and a century of warfare after its downfall the British expanded their influence in the area and established the colony and protectorate of Nigeria in 194 14 incorporating the yua speaking areas in the Southwest Standard Yoda was put together in the 1850s by Samuel A Crowther a Yuba Nigerian and the first Anglican Bishop in West Africa he published a grammar of standard Yuba and translated the Bible into it as well standard Yoda was based on a number of dialects but mainly all ya and I dialects its orthography has gone through several changes since then it's written in a Latin based alphabet but it makes use of a number of accent markings and diacritics and that brings us to an important feature of Yuba yua has from the French word for train station so after just a few basic examples you can see that French has had a significant influence on luxembourgish vocabulary also notice that cognate verbs have slightly different forms in either language pronunciation and orthography Luxemburg has some features of phology that differ from those of standard German and some of the differences are reflected in luxembourgish orthography one important feature is the EA rule words that end in N normally drop the N if the next word begins with a consonant other than n DT Z or H we saw this before in the word May in the sentence this is a feature of the spoken language that arose naturally not only in Mosel franconian dialects but to varying degrees in all of the western group of high German dialects including West Central German high franconian and alanic dialects in luxemburgish the EA rule also affects the written language because the spelling reflects pronunciation but it doesn't affect all words in fact it doesn't affect most nouns and adjectives luxembourgish has both stressed personal pronouns and unstressed personal pronouns in which some sounds are dropped or reduced in lots of languages unstressed vowels are reduced but in luxembourgish the reduced pronunciation is reflected in the standard spelling for example the first singular dative pronoun meaning to me Mia and the third person singular masculine nominative accusative pronoun meaning he grammar while standard German has four noun cases luxembourgish has just two well you could say that there are three nominative accusative and dative but the nominative and accusative have the same form aside from different singular and plural forms the nouns themselves don't change depending on case only determiners such as these definite articles and adjectives this is the same as in standard German the man note that the masculine nominative accusative form and the plural dative form can either end in N or not end in in in accordance with the EA rule that we heard about before for the masculine nominative and accusative compare with meaning the dog and for the plural dative compare with meaning to the dogs the luxembourgish genitive is normally expressed with the dative case plus a possessive adjective there normally isn't a separate genitive form for example the meaning the man's dog word for word it's to the man his dog this isn't limited to luxemburgish and occurs in various dialects throughout the German speaking regions but but not in standard German in standard German there's a separate genitive case there's a difference in comparative forms between luxembourgish and standard German in luxembourgish the comparative form usually uses me meaning more rather than an ER form let's take for example the sentence meaning she's taller than you word for word she is more tall as you and it's standard German is literally she is taller as you note that while German has a cognate of V which is v in standard German it wouldn't be used in a comparison sentence like this it's used in an equal comparative like as tall as in some German dialects though V or its dialectal equivalent might be used just like in luxembourgish also note that the personal pronouns in either language are similar if we look at all the personal pronouns of both languages we can see that they're mostly similar with some differences in form the third person masculine and NE forms the first person plural and the second person plural are different though the first person plural pronoun Mia or similar can be found in other German languages like in dialects of spoken Swiss German and Bavarian in which it's m but this luxembourgish sentence is polite and formal in a casual familiar context it would be note the different personal pronoun H actually functions as a third person singular neuter pronoun even though it's only used to refer to females there are some slight differences in verb tenses and verb conjugations between luxembourgish and German the past tense is expressed using a compound tense in luxembourgish except for a small number of special common verbs the compound past uses the present perfect form but with past meaning it consists of the auxiliary verb meaning to have or Z meaning to be plus the participle of the main verb for example in luxembourgish a this means I slept literally I have slept hon is the first person singular present form of hon the first person singular present form is the same as the infinitive for most verbs so why is it missing the NN at the end of the infinitive well that's the EA rule again G is the participle form of schen and the standard German sentence meaning I slept SCH is the first person singular predate form of schlafen but in standard German as well this is more of a formal or written form in colloquial speech the present perfect is used like in luxembourgish literally I have slept the conditional some luxembourgish verbs have a simple conditional conjugation but they have mostly disappeared from the language and the conditional is normally expressed using a compound form it uses the conditional form of the auxiliary verb gin which by itself means give or become plus the infinitive of the main verb for example meaning I would sleep in German the conditional is formed using the subjunctive to form but this is very formal much more commonly the compound form is used with the auxiliary verb in its subjunctive two form plus the infinitive of the main verb so it's similar to The Compound conditional in luxembourgish but the auxiliary verb is different note that the auxiliary verb meaning to have and the verb meaning to sleep have different forms and their conjugations are different even though they're cognates and developed from the same root there actually both irregular verbs in both languages so let's compare the conjugations of regular verbs you can see that the verb endings added to the verb base are largely the same in luxembourgish and in standard German but the first person singular and second person singular are slightly different notice again that in luxembourgish the first person singular resembles the infinitive because of its ending here's a sentence meaning you don't drink beer do you in luxembourgish you drink word for word it's you drink not beer right and in standard German word for word it corresponds exactly with the luxembourgish sentence now this is just the present tense but of course such differences in conjugated forms occur in all tenses and verb constructions while we're looking at these sentences notice the luxembourgish negation word K which again loses its final n in the sentence because of the EA rule it's a cognate of the German word K in both languages this word negates nouns the luxembourgish word that negates verbs and adjectives is nut a cognate of German here's a sentence meaning I'm not interested in luxembourgish and in German also notice the different first person singular verb forms in luxembourgish Z and in standard German B the first person singular of Z let's look at a final sentence and break it down if I were you I'd look for a new job first in luxembourgish if I you were would I for myself a new job seek and it's standard German word for word it's in your place would I for myself a new job seek notice that the sentences are worded differently at the beginning with the luxembourgish Clause meaning if I were you and the German Clause meaning in your place but in German it could also be which corresponds exactly to the luxembourgish clause in luxembourgish as well you could use a phrase corresponding with the German phrase andat meaning in your place notice the conditional verb in the second Clause again it's the compound conditional but again notice the different auxiliary verbs notice again that most of the luxembourgish and German words are cognates with slightly different forms and pronunciation this indefinite article and this adjective are in the feminine singular accusative form just like the German equivalent this word abish is clearly a cognate of the German word arites and both are feminine all in all the luxembourgish and standard German sentences are very similar but with lots of little differences as you can see luxembourgish is closely related to standard German which makes sense since it's based on a dialect that's part of the high German dialect Continuum from which standard German arose speakers of very similar Mosel franconian dialects across the border in Germany can easily understand luxemburgish and feel like they're speaking the same language though they may be momentarily thrown off by some of the French lone words but luxembourgish is considered its own the turkic language family is a huge family of languages with over 170 million native speakers stretching all the way from europe to northeast asia there are many different languages in this family including turkish azerbaijani or azeri turkmen kazakh tatar and wigar among others interestingly i've spoken with people from turkey who tell me that turkic is a single language some of them said they can understand every single turkic language to a large degree but others say that their comprehension is limited until they get enough exposure to the language the level of mutual intelligibility is often highest for certain languages that are geographically close to each other like turkish and azerbaijani which are said to be anywhere from 65 to 90 mutually intelligible azerbaijani speakers often understand turkish better than the other way around because they see lots of turkish tv shows turkish speakers tend to need several weeks of exposure in order to comprehend what azerbaijani speakers are saying quite well this may also depend on the region and dialect group north azerbaijani is spoken in azerbaijan and south azerbaijani is spoken in some parts of iran turkish and azerbaijani are both members of the oz branch of turkic speakers of oau's turkic began arriving in large numbers in both present-day turkey and present-day azerbaijan as well as northwestern iran in the 11th century when the seljuk empire conquered the area then their speech began to diverge into various regional and local dialects especially since they usually lived under different rulers the ottoman empire had a standardized literary form of turkish known as ottoman turkish which was highly influenced by persian and arabic and the literary language used in azerbaijan was quite similar after the ottoman empire fell turkey removed much of the persian and arabic vocabulary from literary turkish creating a new standard form of turkish that favored words of turkic origin this never ## 46. The Guarani Language

since the colonization of the Americas a huge number of indigenous languages have disappeared and of the remaining ones most are endangered but one language that has been remarkably resilient is guani also known by its endonym guani is not just an official language of Paraguay alongside Spanish but it's also spoken by the majority of the population there including a large number of non- indigenous people varieties of guani are spoken by smaller communities in the neighboring countries of B IA Argentina and Brazil guani is a member of the tupi guani branch of the tupan language family it's more precisely a group of closely related languages some of which form a dialect Continuum with each other but some varieties have low Mutual intelligibility the most widely spoken variety is paraguayan guani which has an estimated 6 to 7 million speakers protot toan the language from which all tupan language is developed is thought to have been spoken in the Meda River Basin in the Brazilian region of honia near the Bolivian border migrations from this region brought tupan to various parts of South America where it began to diverge into different languages including guani not much is known about guani before Spanish and Portuguese colonists began to arrive in the 16th century because it was not a written language the first people to document and describe it were Jesuit missionaries Spanish colonists established the city of aunion in the year 1537 but because of its isolated location and lack of Natural Resources few Spanish people migrated there after it was initially established the Spanish settlers were almost all men who married indigenous guani women their children who became the Elites in the following Generations were bilingual in guani and Spanish which secured a place for guadan in paraguayan society if the Spanish had set up a full Administration there and a bigger Spanish population had been brought over the Spanish language would have likely dominated and guani would be limited to a rural IND ous minority as happened with indigenous languages elsewhere in Latin America the fact that they didn't see aunion as very important and neglected it probably helped guadan to survive as a majority language Jesuit missionaries also helped the language survive they built Jesuit missions known as reductions a type of settlement for indigenous guadan people that operated autonomously from Spanish colonialist rule of course their goal was colonialist in the sense that they wanted to convert guadan people to Catholicism and Europe an way of life but they used the guadan language in their efforts and they developed a system of writing the language they also created a standardized form of the language and compiled guadan dictionaries grammars and textbooks later when Spain forced the Jesuit missionaries to leave monolingual guadan speakers from the missions migrated throughout the country including to the cities after Paraguay gained its independence from Spain in 1811 the country's first dictator used guadan as a nationalist rally and cry and he also prohibited the small number of Spanish Colonials from marrying each other which essentially resulted in forced race mixing and more bilingualism subsequent governments not only reversed that coercive policy but they also repressed the guadan language and banned it in schools though governments used guadan to inspire nationalistic pride in times of War of course how convenient by the middle of the 20th century populist politicians began speaking of guadan more favorably to appeal to the guadan speaking majority in 1967 it was made a national language though Spanish remained the only official language and in 1992 guani was made an official language alongside Spanish according to the 2012 census results 46.3% of households used both guani and Spanish and 34% used only guani that means over 80% of paraguayans speak guani the majority of guani speakers use a form of the language called yop that involves heavy codee switching with Spanish the word yop means mixture unlike the simple integration of Spanish lone words referred to as y refers to actual code switching or code mixing that includes elements of Spanish grammar the form of guani used in schools is not but a more traditional and literary form of the language let's take a look at some of the features of guani the orthography of guani is based on the Latin orthography used for Spanish with a few extra characters that represent guadan sounds that don't appear in Spanish all syllables consist of a vowel or a consonant plus a vowel no syllables end in consonants at least for Native guani words for example meaning hello meaning how are you and this is often used as a greeting instead of meaning fine or good and you I'm fine too guani has six pairs of vowels six or vowels plus their nasal equivalent all voiced stops and the one arriate are prenasalized they only occur before oral vowels their alones are the nasal consonants which occur before nasal vowels replacing an oral consonant with a nasal consonant to match adjacent nasal sounds is an important feature of guani guadan has nasal Harmony if there's a stressed nasal vowel or a nasal consonant its nasality spreads to adjacent parts of the word this happens with certain prefixes for example one such prefix is the first person plural inclusive subject pronoun prefix yaah which comes before an oral verb stem before a nasal verb stem it becomes so there's meaning we laugh or we laughed and there's meaning we speak or we spoke so as a little side note here guani has a distinction between we which includes the listener and we which excludes The Listener guani is a glutena meaning that rather than adding additional words to a sentence to show grammatical distinctions multiple prefixes and suffixes can be added instead here's an example this phrase means see you later the root is this verb meaning see the subject is prefixed before the verb root we see then another prefix is added between the subject prefix and verb root to show reciprocation we see each other then an adverb meaning until is added after the verb until we see each other or just see you later also nouns they take suffixes to show number and case but not gender this means cat and this means cats is an optional plural marker when it comes after a nasal vowel it becomes this means child and this means children the child loves the cat notice at the end of the word for cat this is a suffix that indicates the accusative case for animate objects and it can also show the locative case like for a destination or location some other such suffixes are we which indicates the ablative case like from and the or as a separate word which indicates accompaniment like with syntax notice the word order of this sentence SV this is the most common word order in basic declarative sentences but in reality the word order is flexible and it's fairly common for the subject to come after the verb and for objects to come before the verb notice that there's no definite article no word meaning the there's no native definite article in guani but the Spanish definite articles LA and lo are often used in Daily conversation though this would be considered y possession what if we want to say the child loves his cat or her cat e is a prefix that functions as a possessive adjective this one being third person the other possessive adjectives are separate words that aren't prefixed like this this means your name means name specifically a given name not a family name means you what's your name my name is Juan when the possessor is an actual noun it still comes before the thing that is possessed means my father's name means my father and you they're the same as the possessives that we saw earlier and the same as the Standalone subject pronouns except for the third person for example I I walk or I walked this is active I remember or I if you're a kiwi you're probably tired of hearing about sheep lord of the rings and people asking you where your country is you're probably also tired of people hearing your accent and asking where are you from australia while the english spoken in new zealand does share a lot of features with australian english it's a distinct variety of english that has its own unique features as well between 1788 and 1841 new zealand was loosely part of the colony of new south wales which later became part of australia the earliest european settlers in new zealand were traders and whalers from new south wales who set up trading stations they were followed by missionaries from england beginning in 1814. the colony of new zealand was declared as a separate entity in 1841 and britain began to carry out a process of planned settlement most migrants came from england wales scotland and ireland but many did not come directly a significant number arrived from australia so some of them likely brought their children who had been born in australia this could have had an effect on the way other children in the colony spoke and influenced the emerging new zealand accent according to the census of 1871 the vast majority of migrants were from britain and ireland but an inordinate number were scottish or irish 27.3 percent and 22 respectively these particular percentages could have also had an influence on the emerging accent new zealand english resembles australian english more than any other variety which may be a result of that early australian settlement a somewhat similar mix of migrant accents and continuous contact between new zealand and australia ever since those days the kiwi accent not all kiwi accents are the same there are basically two accents a cultivated accent which more closely resembles british received pronunciation and a broad accent which includes more distinctly kiwi features but a spectrum of accents exists in between the two with those somewhere in the middle range being considered general new zealand english as is typical in england and australia accents of new zealand are generally non-rhodic meaning that r is not pronounced at the end of a syllable but is rendered as a vowel for example i say beer but kiwis say b the main thing that distinguishes the english of new zealand from australian english and other varieties is its vowels some of them shifted during the 20th century and these changes are more pronounced in broad accents most strikingly the original short front vowels shifted these changes lead to some interesting contrasts listen to these examples in my accent and the new zealand accent chat chat mess miss sit sit the shift in kiwi vowels was a chain shift meaning the first vowel began to sound like the second one and the second one began to sound like the third one this might result in a misunderstanding if you aren't aware of the shift sex sex sex seeks six sucks please be mindful of this when a kiwi is talking about christmas sacks or playing the sacks i find that when listening to kiwis with a particularly broad accent i have to sort of translate the vowels in my head to make sure that i'm understanding the right word aussies love to make fun of the third vowel shift accusing kiwis of saying fashion chops in aussie english that same vowel has actually shifted slightly in the opposite direction so kiwis love to accuse aussies of saying fish and chips of course both pronunciations are exaggerated before i mention the shift from a to e but it's worth pointing out that kiwi english underwent the trap bath split like british and australian english but unlike north american english the trap bath split means that the early modern english vowel a became long ah in received pronunciation and long ah in new zealand pronunciation before these specific consonants before other consonants it's a in received pronunciation and e in new zealand so if you're a speaker of north american english some of the words that you pronounce with an a will have e in kiwi english but others will have ah another more recent shift in kiwi vowels is that the vowels in near and square are pronounced the same by some speakers near near square square it's not fair it's not fair another thing you might hear in kiwi english is that past participles like grown and known are sometimes pronounced as growin and knowin with a vowel inserted that probably developed by analogy with other past participles like written or spoken which have a vowel in that position this pronunciation is used by around 50 of new zealanders while there are different kiwi accents based on social and economic factors for the most part there aren't regional variations an exception is the regional accent of southland and part of neighboring otago where more than half of the original settlers were from scotland one characteristic of this accent is the alveolar trill or rolling r sound which occurs after vowels these days in particular after words with the vowel in nurse this is a feature that once faded and became less common but then rebounded and started to become more common again there's also a slightly different accent that is specific to some kiwis of maori background it's particularly broad in its vowels it's sometimes rhodic like the southland accent the t sound is sometimes unaspirated and voiced z is
